gpt-6-luna/03-infra-management/RESPONSE.md

Review response

Highest-risk original problems

  • The database password was hard-coded as admin:password, and Postgres, Redis, and the API were all published directly on host ports. That exposed data services and bypassed nginx.
  • The API ran Flask's development server as root, despite gunicorn already being listed as a dependency. The Docker build ignored requirements.txt and installed unpinned package names; all service images also used mutable latest tags.
  • There were no health checks, readiness-based startup dependencies, or persistent Postgres volume. A simple start order could leave the API connecting before its dependencies were ready, and database data could be lost when the container was replaced.
  • nginx forwarded requests without client/protocol headers or explicit connection and response timeouts.

Changes and verification

The API now runs Gunicorn as UID/GID 10001 with an exact, fully pinned Python dependency set. Compose keeps only nginx host-published, separates edge and internal data networks, persists Postgres in pgdata, and defines health checks, health-based startup dependencies, restart policies, bounded logs, resource limits, and graceful stop periods. nginx forwards the client and scheme headers and sets bounded proxy timeouts. .env.example, local ignore rules, RUNBOOK.md, and this review record cover local secrets and operations.

Static review confirmed that Compose contains no published API, Postgres, or Redis ports, no embedded database password, and no latest image tags. The configuration was checked with docker compose --env-file .env.example config --quiet; this validates Compose parsing without starting containers. No containers were started. Runtime behavior could not be checked because this directory contains no API implementation; the default Gunicorn target is app:app, and its readiness check requires GET /healthz to return HTTP 200.

gpt-6-luna/03-infra-management/RUNBOOK.md

Runbook

Start and verify

The API image expects a Flask WSGI target in APP_MODULE (default app:app) and a fast GET /healthz route that returns HTTP 200 when the API is ready. The supplied directory has no API source, so provide that module and route in the application before expecting the API container to become healthy.

  1. Copy .env.example to .env. Replace POSTGRES_PASSWORD with the output of openssl rand -hex 32; hexadecimal keeps the value safe inside the generated DATABASE_URL. Keep the other settings appropriate for the deployment.
  2. Start the stack with docker compose up -d --build.
  3. Check docker compose ps for healthy services, then verify nginx and the proxied API with curl -fsS http://localhost:8080/_nginx_health and curl -fsS http://localhost:8080/healthz. If HTTP_PORT was changed, use that port. Inspect failures with docker compose logs --tail=100 api db cache proxy.

Only nginx is published to the host. The API is reachable by nginx over the edge network, while Postgres and Redis are reachable by the API over the internal backend network. Redis is a volatile cache and is not backed up. Compose health checks gate startup order; they do not provide orchestration, traffic failover, or automatic recovery from application-level failures.

Backup and restore

Make and protect a custom-format Postgres backup before upgrades or data changes. Store backups outside the project directory and encrypt them when they leave the host:

umask 077
docker compose exec -T db sh -c 'pg_dump -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -d "$POSTGRES_DB" -Fc' > /secure/backups/backup-$(date +%F).dump

To restore, stop API writes, then restore the selected dump into the configured database. This replaces objects present in the archive; confirm the target and retain a separate pre-restore backup first.

docker compose stop api
docker compose exec -T db sh -c 'pg_restore --clean --if-exists --no-owner -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -d "$POSTGRES_DB"' < /secure/backups/backup-YYYY-MM-DD.dump
docker compose up -d api proxy
docker compose ps

The named pgdata volume survives docker compose down; docker compose down -v removes it and must not be used when the database should be retained.

Secrets

Keep .env local and out of version control. It is excluded from this directory's Git and Docker build contexts; commit only .env.example. Do not put real values in Compose files or logs. Compose passes the database password as a container environment variable, which is visible to administrators of the Docker host. For production, source it from an approved secret manager and adapt the API to consume mounted secrets if the deployment requires them. Changing POSTGRES_PASSWORD in .env does not rotate the role password in an already-initialized data volume; rotate the database role and API secret together.

Rollback

Keep the last known-good application and configuration revision plus a pre-deployment database dump. To roll back, check out that revision and run docker compose up -d --build, then verify the health checks and API. Do not remove the Postgres volume. If a schema change is incompatible with the old application, stop writes and restore the pre-deployment dump before bringing the old API back up. Compose restart policies restart failed containers but do not roll out versions or undo schema changes.

gpt-6-luna/03-infra-management/TASK.md

Task 03 — Infrastructure management: harden a small deployment

Review and improve the supplied Dockerfile, compose.yaml, and nginx.conf for a small Python API behind nginx with Postgres and Redis. Do not add Kubernetes or unrelated services.

Goals:

  • No embedded credentials or public database/cache ports.
  • The API runs as a non-root user with a production server and reproducible dependency installation.
  • Add meaningful health checks and startup dependencies based on health.
  • Persist Postgres data, make internal service communication explicit, and add sensible restart/logging/resource settings without pretending Compose is a full orchestrator.
  • Proxy headers and timeouts should be appropriate for a JSON API.
  • Keep local operation straightforward through .env.example.

Deliver corrected configuration and a concise RUNBOOK.md covering startup, health verification, backup/restore, secret handling, and rollback. Write RESPONSE.md listing the highest-risk original problems and verification you performed. Work only in this directory; do not actually start containers.

#   openssl rand -hex 32

POSTGRES_PASSWORD=REPLACE_WITH_RANDOM_HEX_SECRET
POSTGRES_DB=app

# Host port for nginx. Postgres, Redis, and the API are not published.
HTTP_PORT=8080

# Python WSGI module, in Gunicorn's module:variable form.
APP_MODULE=app:app

services:
  proxy:
    image: nginx:1.29-alpine
    ports:
      - "${HTTP_PORT:-8080}:80"
    volumes:
      - ./nginx.conf:/etc/nginx/nginx.conf:ro
    depends_on:
      api:
        condition: service_healthy
    networks:
      - edge
    restart: unless-stopped
    stop_grace_period: 10s
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "wget -q -O /dev/null http://127.0.0.1/_nginx_health || exit 1"]
      interval: 30s
      timeout: 5s
      retries: 3
      start_period: 5s
    logging:
      driver: json-file
      options:
        max-size: "10m"
        max-file: "3"
    cpus: 0.25
    mem_limit: 128m
    pids_limit: 100

  api:
    build:
      context: .
      dockerfile: Dockerfile
    environment:
      APP_MODULE: "${APP_MODULE:-app:app}"
      DATABASE_URL: "postgresql://${POSTGRES_USER:?Set POSTGRES_USER in .env}:${POSTGRES_PASSWORD:?Set POSTGRES_PASSWORD in .env}@db:5432/${POSTGRES_DB:?Set POSTGRES_DB in .env}"
      REDIS_URL: redis://cache:6379/0
    depends_on:
      db:
        condition: service_healthy
      cache:
        condition: service_healthy
    networks:
      - edge
      - backend
    init: true
    restart: unless-stopped
    stop_grace_period: 30s
    healthcheck:
      test:
        - CMD

      POSTGRES_DB: "${POSTGRES_DB:?Set POSTGRES_DB in .env}"
    volumes:
      - pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data
    networks:
      - backend
    restart: unless-stopped
    stop_grace_period: 1m
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -U \"$${POSTGRES_USER}\" -d \"$${POSTGRES_DB}\""]
      interval: 10s
      timeout: 5s
      retries: 5
      start_period: 20s

gpt-6-luna/03-infra-management/Dockerfile

FROM python:3.13-slim-bookworm

ENV PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 \
    PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1 \
    PIP_DISABLE_PIP_VERSION_CHECK=1

WORKDIR /app

RUN addgroup --system --gid 10001 app \
    && adduser --system --uid 10001 --ingroup app --home /app app

COPY requirements.txt ./
RUN python -m pip install --no-cache-dir --requirement requirements.txt

COPY --chown=app:app . .

USER 10001:10001
EXPOSE 8000

CMD ["sh", "-c", "exec gunicorn --bind 0.0.0.0:8000 --workers 2 --threads 2 --timeout 60 --access-logfile - --error-logfile - \"${APP_MODULE:-app:app}\""]

gpt-6-luna/03-infra-management/nginx.conf

worker_processes auto;

events {
    worker_connections 1024;
}

http {
    include /etc/nginx/mime.types;
    default_type application/octet-stream;
    server_tokens off;
    resolver 127.0.0.11 valid=10s ipv6=off;

    server {
        listen 80;
        client_max_body_size 1m;

        location = /_nginx_health {
            access_log off;
            default_type text/plain;
            return 200 "ok\n";
        }

        location / {
            set $api_backend api:8000;
            proxy_pass http://$api_backend;
            proxy_http_version 1.1;
            proxy_set_header Connection "";

            proxy_set_header Host $host;
            proxy_set_header X-Real-IP $remote_addr;
            proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $remote_addr;

            proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;

            proxy_connect_timeout 5s;
            proxy_send_timeout 30s;
            proxy_read_timeout 60s;
        }
    }

glm-5.3/03-infra-management/RESPONSE.md

Response — Task 03: Infrastructure hardening

Highest-risk problems in the original configuration

  1. Credentials in version control — compose.yaml embedded admin / password twice (db environment and DATABASE_URL). Anyone with repo access has production DB credentials; anyone watching a CI log sees them too.
  2. Database and cache published to the host — 5432:5432 and 6379:6379 were bound on all host interfaces. Redis had no authentication, so on many networks it was remotely reachable and writable (a well-known RCE path via module loading). Postgres was reachable with the weak committed password.
  3. Flask development server as the production process — flask run + FLASK_ENV=development means the Werkzeug dev server (no concurrency tuning, debug-oriented behavior) handling real traffic.
  4. No readiness gating — depends_on only ordered container startup, so the API routinely booted before Postgres accepted connections and crashed or served 500s.
  5. No Postgres persistence — no volume on /var/lib/postgresql/data; docker compose down destroyed all application data.
  6. Unreproducible build — pip install flask psycopg2 redis ignored the pinned requirements.txt (floating versions, different image per build); all service images were :latest.
  7. Container ran as root with COPY . . — no .dockerignore, so a local .env (or any future secret file) would be baked into image layers, readable by anyone with image pull access, running as root.
  8. No restart policy, no log limits — a crash stayed down; json-file logs grew unbounded until the host disk filled.
  9. Unconfigured proxy — nginx forwarded nothing (Host, X-Real-IP, X-Forwarded-For, X-Forwarded-Proto missing, so the API cannot see client IPs/protocol), used HTTP/1.0-style upstream connections, and had default 60s proxy timeouts with no body-size limit; version leaked via Server: header.

Changes made

  • compose.yaml: all secrets interpolated from .env with :? fail-fast errors; no published ports for db/cache/api (expose only documents internal ports); named db_data volume; health checks for all four services with depends_on: condition: service_healthy; split frontend / backend networks, backend marked internal: true (no egress, and the proxy cannot reach db/cache at all); restart: unless-stopped; rotated json-file logging; memory/CPU limits; no-new-privileges on every service; pinned image tags (nginx:1.27-alpine, postgres:17, redis:7-alpine).
  • Dockerfile: pinned base (python:3.13-slim-bookworm); installs the pinned requirements.txt (dependency layer cached separately); non-root app user; gunicorn production CMD (exec form, tuned workers); dev-server env removed.
  • nginx.conf: proxy headers, HTTP/1.1 + keepalive upstream, connect/send/read timeouts tuned for a JSON API, client_max_body_size 1m, server_tokens off, gzip for JSON, and a proxy-local /health endpoint for the compose healthcheck.
  • New files: .dockerignore (keeps .env, VCS files, caches out of the image), .gitignore (keeps .env out of git), expanded .env.example (all variables with generation guidance), RUNBOOK.md (startup, health verification, backup/restore, secret handling incl. password rotation, rollback).

Verification performed

Offline, without starting any containers:

  • docker compose config (Compose v5.5.0, client-side validation) renders the full stack cleanly with test values: only the proxy publishes a port, db/cache/api are expose-only, all health checks, resource limits, network attachments, and the db_data volume resolve as intended.
  • With .env absent, docker compose config exits non-zero with actionable "required variable ... is missing" errors — confirmed the fail-fast secret handling works.
  • A Python structural check over nginx.conf: balanced braces, every directive terminated, and all required proxy-header/timeout directives present. (No nginx binary is available here, so nginx -t could not be run — full syntax validation should be done at first up.)
  • Dockerfile checked programmatically: exec-form CMD parses as valid JSON, references gunicorn, and the file contains no dev-server remnants; non-root user and --no-cache-dir install confirmed.
  • No credential strings remain in Dockerfile/compose.yaml/nginx.conf; .env.example contains placeholders only. No container was started and no network was used.

Assumptions (adjust if wrong)

  • The Flask app is exposed as app in app.py (gunicorn app:app) and serves GET /health with 2xx. Both are one-line changes (Dockerfile CMD / api healthcheck) and are called out in RUNBOOK.md.
  • Versioned image tags were chosen as stable majors; pin to exact digests in a real release pipeline.

glm-5.3/03-infra-management/RUNBOOK.md

Runbook — app stack (nginx / API / Postgres / Redis)

A single-host Docker Compose deployment. nginx is the only publicly reachable service; the API, Postgres, and Redis live on internal networks (backend is internal: true, so it has no internet egress either).

client -> proxy (nginx, :80) -> api (gunicorn :5000) -> db / cache
         frontend network            frontend + backend      backend

Prerequisites

  • Docker Engine + Docker Compose v2 (docker compose version)
  • A .env file in this directory (see below)

First-time startup

cp .env.example .env
$EDITOR .env          # set POSTGRES_PASSWORD (openssl rand -hex 24)
docker compose up -d --build
docker compose ps     # all four services should report (healthy)

Compose interpolates credentials from .env and refuses to start with a clear error if any required variable is missing. The API waits for db and cache to pass their health checks before starting; the proxy waits for the API.

The application must expose GET /health returning 2xx — the compose healthcheck for api and the gunicorn entrypoint assume this. If your Flask module/object is not app:app, change the gunicorn argument in the Dockerfile.

Health verification

# Container-level (healthchecks from compose.yaml)
docker compose ps

# Proxy is serving (nginx answers this locally, no upstream involved)
curl -fsS http://localhost/health

# End-to-end through nginx to the API
curl -fsS http://localhost/<any-real-api-route>

# Component checks from inside the stack
docker compose exec db pg_isready -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -d "$POSTGRES_DB"
docker compose exec cache redis-cli ping
docker compose exec api python - <<'PY'
import os, urllib.request
urllib.request.urlopen("http://127.0.0.1:5000/health", timeout=3)
print("api healthy")
PY

# Logs (json-file driver, rotated at 10m x 3 files per container)
docker compose logs --tail=100 api
docker compose logs -f proxy

Backup / restore (Postgres)

Data persists in the named volume db_data, so it survives docker compose down.

First load your .env into the shell (the commands below expand $POSTGRES_USER / $POSTGRES_DB locally):

set -a; source .env; set +a

# Backup (custom-format dump; portable and compressible)
docker compose exec -T db pg_dump -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -Fc "$POSTGRES_DB" > backup.dump

# Restore into an empty database, or clean existing objects first
docker compose exec -T db pg_restore -U "$POSTGRES_USER" --clean --if-exists \
  -d "$POSTGRES_DB" < backup.dump

# Physical fallback: snapshot the volume (stop writes first)
docker compose stop api
docker run --rm -v <project>_db_data:/data -v "$PWD":/backup alpine \
  tar czf /backup/db_data.tgz -C /data .
docker compose start api

Store dumps off-host. Restore order: stop api (so nothing writes during restore), restore, then docker compose up -d.

Secret handling

  • All credentials live in .env, which is git-ignored and excluded from the image build via .dockerignore. Nothing is hardcoded in compose.yaml / Dockerfile.
  • Generate the DB password with openssl rand -hex 24. Use only characters that are safe inside a URI (hex is), or URL-encode them — the password is interpolated into DATABASE_URL.
  • Password rotation: changing POSTGRES_PASSWORD in .env does NOT affect an already-initialized volume. Rotate like this:
    docker compose exec db psql -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -d "$POSTGRES_DB" \
  -c "ALTER USER \"$POSTGRES_USER\" WITH PASSWORD '<new>';"
# then update .env and restart the API
docker compose up -d api
  • For production, prefer a real secret store (Vault, cloud secret manager) or at least Docker secrets / an orchestrator; .env is a convenience for local/small deployments, not an access-control mechanism. Restrict file permissions (chmod 600 .env).

Rollback

The state that matters is db_data plus the deployed configuration.

# 1. Roll back the configuration to the previous version
git checkout <previous-tag-or-commit> -- Dockerfile compose.yaml nginx.conf

# 2. Rebuild and restart (data volume is untouched)
docker compose up -d --build

# 3. If the data schema also regressed, restore the matching dump
docker compose exec -T db pg_restore -U "$POSTGRES_USER" --clean --if-exists \
  -d "$POSTGRES_DB" < backup.dump

Useful operations:

docker compose down          # stop and remove containers; db_data survives
docker compose down --volumes   # DANGER: deletes Postgres data permanently
docker compose restart api   # bounce a single service

Take a fresh pg_dump before any rollback that touches the database.

Limits of this setup (Compose is not an orchestrator)

  • Single host only; if the machine dies, everything is down. restart: unless-stopped restarts crashed containers, but there is no rescheduling.
  • Resource limits are enforced by the kernel where supported (cgroups), but there is no autoscaling or cluster self-healing.
  • Log rotation is per-container (10 MB x 3); aggregate logs elsewhere if you need retention.
  • Backups are manual — schedule them via cron/host tooling if this stops being a dev deployment.

Task 03 — Infrastructure management: harden a small deployment

Review and improve the supplied Dockerfile, compose.yaml, and nginx.conf for a small Python API behind nginx with Postgres and Redis. Do not add Kubernetes or unrelated services.

Goals:

  • No embedded credentials or public database/cache ports.
  • The API runs as a non-root user with a production server and reproducible dependency installation.
  • Add meaningful health checks and startup dependencies based on health.
  • Persist Postgres data, make internal service communication explicit, and add sensible restart/logging/resource settings without pretending Compose is a full orchestrator.
  • Proxy headers and timeouts should be appropriate for a JSON API.
  • Keep local operation straightforward through .env.example.

Deliver corrected configuration and a concise RUNBOOK.md covering startup, health verification, backup/restore, secret handling, and rollback. Write RESPONSE.md listing the highest-risk original problems and verification you performed. Work only in this directory; do not actually start containers.

# Copy to .env and fill in real values:  cp .env.example .env
# .env must never be committed (see .gitignore).

# PostgreSQL credentials (used by the db service and to build DATABASE_URL).
# Generate a strong password, e.g.:  openssl rand -hex 24
# Keep it alphanumeric-only, or URL-encode special characters, because it is
# embedded in DATABASE_URL by compose.yaml.
POSTGRES_USER=app

# Host port published by nginx (the only externally reachable port).

glm-5.3/03-infra-management/compose.yaml

# Docker Compose v2+ file. All credentials are interpolated from .env
# (see .env.example); services fail to start with a clear error if missing.
#
# Note: Compose is not an orchestrator. restart policies and resource limits
# are best-effort on a single host; there is no autoscaling, rescheduling,
# or cluster-level self-healing here.

x-logging: &default-logging
  driver: json-file
  options:
    max-size: "10m"
    max-file: "3"

services:
  proxy:
    image: nginx:1.27-alpine
    restart: unless-stopped
    ports:
      # Only externally published port in the stack.
      - "${HTTP_PORT:-80}:80"
    volumes:
      - ./nginx.conf:/etc/nginx/nginx.conf:ro
    depends_on:
      api:
        condition: service_healthy
    networks:
      - frontend
    security_opt:
      - no-new-privileges:true
    logging: *default-logging
    deploy:
      resources:
        limits:
          memory: 128M
          cpus: "0.50"
    healthcheck:
      # nginx serves /health itself (see nginx.conf), so this check
      # reflects the proxy, not the upstream API.
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "wget -q --spider http://localhost/health || exit 1"]
      interval: 30s
      timeout: 5s
      retries: 3
      start_period: 10s

  api:
    build: .
    restart: unless-stopped
    # Internal only: reached through the proxy, never published.
    expose:
      - "5000"
    environment:
      DATABASE_URL: postgresql://${POSTGRES_USER}:${POSTGRES_PASSWORD}@db:5432/${POSTGRES_DB}
      REDIS_URL: redis://cache:6379/0
    depends_on:
      db:
        condition: service_healthy
      cache:
        condition: service_healthy
    networks:
      - frontend
      - backend
    security_opt:
      - no-new-privileges:true
    logging: *default-logging
    deploy:
      resources:
        limits:
          memory: 512M
          cpus: "1.00"
    healthcheck:
      # App must expose GET /health returning 2xx (see RUNBOOK).
      test: ["CMD", "python", "-c", "import urllib.request; urllib.request.urlopen('http://127.0.0.1:5000/health', timeout=3)"]
      interval: 15s
      timeout: 5s
      retries: 5
      start_period: 15s

  db:
    image: postgres:17
    restart: unless-stopped
    expose:
      - "5432"
    environment:
      POSTGRES_USER: ${POSTGRES_USER:?set POSTGRES_USER in .env}
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD:?set POSTGRES_PASSWORD in .env}
      POSTGRES_DB: ${POSTGRES_DB:?set POSTGRES_DB in .env}
    volumes:
      - db_data:/var/lib/postgresql/data
    networks:
      - backend
    security_opt:
      - no-new-privileges:true
    logging: *default-logging
    deploy:
      resources:
        limits:
          memory: 512M
          cpus: "1.00"
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -U \"$$POSTGRES_USER\" -d \"$$POSTGRES_DB\""]
      interval: 10s
      timeout: 5s
      retries: 5
      start_period: 10s

  cache:
    image: redis:7-alpine
    restart: unless-stopped
    expose:
      - "6379"
    networks:
      - backend
    security_opt:
      - no-new-privileges:true
    logging: *default-logging
    deploy:
      resources:
        limits:
          memory: 256M
          cpus: "0.50"
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD", "redis-cli", "ping"]
      interval: 10s
      timeout: 3s
      retries: 5
      start_period: 5s

volumes:
  db_data:

networks:
  frontend:
  backend:
    # No default gateway: db/cache cannot reach the internet, and the proxy
    # cannot reach them at all (only api is attached to backend).
    internal: true

glm-5.3/03-infra-management/Dockerfile

FROM python:3.13-slim-bookworm

ENV PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 \
    PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1 \
    PIP_NO_CACHE_DIR=1 \
    PIP_DISABLE_PIP_VERSION_CHECK=1

WORKDIR /app

# Unprivileged user for running the application.
RUN groupadd --system app \
    && useradd --system --gid app --home-dir /app --shell /usr/sbin/nologin app

# Install pinned dependencies first so the layer is reused unless
# requirements.txt changes (fast, reproducible builds).
COPY requirements.txt ./
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

# Copy application code with correct ownership, then drop privileges.
COPY --chown=app:app . ./
USER app

EXPOSE 5000

# Production WSGI server. Assumes the Flask app object is exposed as `app`
# in `app.py` at the project root; adjust the module path if not.
CMD ["gunicorn", \
     "--bind", "0.0.0.0:5000", \
     "--workers", "2", \
     "--threads", "4", \
     "--worker-tmp-dir", "/dev/shm", \
     "--timeout", "60", \
     "--graceful-timeout", "30", \
     "--access-logfile", "-", \
     "app:app"]

glm-5.3/03-infra-management/nginx.conf

worker_processes auto;

events {
  worker_connections 1024;
}

http {
  include /etc/nginx/mime.types;
  default_type application/json;

  sendfile on;
  tcp_nopush on;
  keepalive_timeout 30s;

  # Don't advertise the nginx version in headers or error pages.
  server_tokens off;

  # JSON API: payloads are small; reject oversized bodies early.
  client_max_body_size 1m;
  client_body_timeout 10s;
  client_header_timeout 10s;

  gzip on;
  gzip_min_length 256;
  gzip_types application/json;
  gzip_proxied any;

  # Keepalive connections to the upstream (works with proxy_set_header Connection "").
  proxy_http_version 1.1;

  server {
    listen 80;
    server_name _;

    # Timeouts tuned for a JSON API: fail fast when connecting,
    # but allow slow handlers up to 30s.
    proxy_connect_timeout 5s;
    proxy_send_timeout 30s;
    proxy_read_timeout 30s;

    # Proxy-local health endpoint. Used by the compose healthcheck and
    # by the operator; it deliberately does NOT hit the upstream.
    location = /health {
      access_log off;
      return 200 "ok\n";
    }

    location / {
      proxy_pass http://api:5000;
      proxy_set_header Host $host;
      proxy_set_header X-Real-IP $remote_addr;
      proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
      proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;
      proxy_set_header Connection "";
    }
  }
}

muse/03-infra-management/RESPONSE.md

Response — highest-risk original problems and verification

Highest-risk problems in the original configuration

  1. Hardcoded credentials in compose.yaml (admin:password, both in DATABASE_URL and the db environment). Committed secrets leak via git history and are readable by anyone with repo access. Fixed by sourcing all credentials from .env (required :? interpolation fails fast when unset) with only placeholders in .env.example.
  2. Database and cache ports published to the host (5432, 6379, plus the API's 5000). Postgres/Redis were reachable from the network with weak known credentials. Fixed by publishing only nginx:80; db/cache sit on an internal: true backend network and the API is expose-only.
  3. Postgres data not persisted (no volume). Any down, upgrade, or host restart silently destroyed all data. Fixed with a named pgdata volume plus backup/restore procedures in RUNBOOK.md.
  4. Development server running as root (flask run with FLASK_ENV=development on the full python:3.13 image). Debug-mode behavior, no process management, and a needless root user maximize blast radius. Fixed with a slim image, dedicated appuser, and gunicorn.
  5. Unreproducible, unpinned builds (:latest images; pip install flask psycopg2 redis ignoring the pinned requirements.txt). Every build could drift or pull a compromised release. Fixed with pinned image tags and pip install -r requirements.txt.
  6. No health checks or ordered startup (plain depends_on, COPY . . before install). The API raced the database on every boot and failures stayed silent. Fixed with per-service health checks and condition: service_healthy dependencies.
  7. Bare nginx proxy (no forwarded headers, no timeouts). The API saw wrong client IPs/scheme and a hung upstream could hold connections forever. Fixed with X-Forwarded-*/Host headers, connect/read/send timeouts, body limits, and a dedicated /nginx-health probe.
  8. Secrets baked into image layers (COPY . . before install, no .dockerignore). A local .env would have been copied into the image. Fixed with .dockerignore excluding .env and install-before-copy layer ordering.

What changed (summary)

  • Dockerfile: slim pinned-base image, non-root appuser, reproducible install from requirements.txt, gunicorn, image-level HEALTHCHECK.
  • compose.yaml: no hardcoded secrets, no public db/cache/api ports, pinned images, pgdata volume, frontend/backend networks (backend internal), health checks + health-gated startup, unless-stopped restarts, bounded logging, modest resource limits.
  • nginx.conf: proxy headers, timeouts, body limits, server_tokens off, upstream with fail thresholds, /nginx-health endpoint.
  • .env.example: documents every variable with placeholder secrets.
  • Added .dockerignore, RUNBOOK.md, RESPONSE.md.

Assumptions (no app source was supplied)

  • API entrypoint is app:app and it serves GET /healthz → 200.
  • Redis stays passwordless on the isolated backend network (documented opt-in for auth in .env.example).

Verification performed (no containers started, no network used)

  • docker compose config with dummy env vars: exit 0; rendered output confirms only proxy publishes a port, db mounts pgdata, backend network is internal, all four health checks and three service_healthy gates present.
  • docker compose config without secrets: fails fast naming the missing variable (Set POSTGRES_USER in .env), exit 1.
  • Secret scan over compose.yaml/Dockerfile/nginx.conf: no literal credentials — only ${POSTGRES_*} references and a .dockerignore comment mention them.
  • :latest and flask run/FLASK_ENV scans: no remaining hits.
  • Dockerfile contains USER appuser, pip install -r requirements.txt, and a gunicorn CMD.
  • nginx.conf: braces balanced (6/6); all required proxy headers, timeouts, client_max_body_size, server_tokens off, upstream, and health endpoint present.
  • Not run (per constraints): docker build, docker compose up, nginx -t (no nginx binary installed); image pulls would require network access.

muse/03-infra-management/RUNBOOK.md

Runbook — small Python API behind nginx (Compose)

Stack: proxy (nginx) → api (gunicorn/Flask) → db (Postgres 16), cache (Redis 7, ephemeral). Single-host Compose deployment.

Assumptions

  • The API entrypoint is app.py exposing Flask object app, and it serves GET /healthz with HTTP 200 when ready (also after DB/Redis checks pass).
  • A .env file exists (see "Startup"). DATABASE_URL/REDIS_URL are built by Compose; only Postgres credentials and the nginx host port are set.

Startup

  1. cp .env.example .env, then set a strong POSTGRES_PASSWORD in .env.
  2. docker compose up -d --build
  3. docker compose ps — every service should reach (healthy).
  4. curl -f http://localhost:<NGINX_HTTP_PORT>/healthz should return 200.

Stop: docker compose stop. Full teardown (containers + networks, data kept in the pgdata volume): docker compose down.

Health verification

  • docker compose ps shows per-service health status.
  • Proxy itself: curl -f http://localhost:<NGINX_HTTP_PORT>/nginx-health
  • End to end: curl -f http://localhost:<NGINX_HTTP_PORT>/healthz
  • Postgres: docker compose exec db pg_isready -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -d "$POSTGRES_DB" (load .env into the shell first, or substitute values literally).
  • Redis: docker compose exec cache redis-cli ping (expect PONG).
  • Logs: docker compose logs --tail=100 <service>.

Startup order is health-gated: api waits for db and cache to be healthy; proxy waits for api. A service stuck in (unhealthy) blocks its dependents — inspect its logs before restarting anything.

Backup / restore

Postgres data lives in the named volume pgdata and survives down and container recreation. It is deleted only by docker compose down -v.

  • Logical backup: docker compose exec -T db pg_dump -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -d "$POSTGRES_DB" > backup-$(date +%F).sql
  • Restore to a running stack: cat backup.sql | docker compose exec -T db psql -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -d "$POSTGRES_DB"
  • Volume-level backup (stack stopped): docker run --rm -v <project>_pgdata:/data -v "$PWD:/out" alpine tar czf /out/pgdata-$(date +%F).tgz -C /data .
  • Verify a backup by restoring into a scratch database and running a smoke query; never treat an unrestored dump as a verified backup.

Redis is a cache with no volume: nothing to back up. It starts empty after recreation by design.

Secret handling

  • Secrets live only in .env (mode 600, never committed). No credentials are hardcoded in compose.yaml, Dockerfile, or nginx.conf.
  • .dockerignore excludes .env so secrets are never baked into image layers via COPY . ..
  • Rotate the Postgres password: update .env, then docker compose exec db psql -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -d "$POSTGRES_DB" -c "ALTER USER \"$POSTGRES_USER\" WITH PASSWORD '<new>'" and docker compose up -d api to pick up the new DATABASE_URL. A restart of db is not needed for the password change itself.
  • Treat any credential once committed to git as compromised: rotate it.

Rollback

  1. Identify the last good state: docker compose config (current rendered config) and your git history for these files.
  2. Code/config rollback: git stash or git checkout -- <files> to the last good commit, then docker compose up -d --build.
  3. Data rollback: restore a pg_dump backup as above. Note restores lose writes made after the backup; there is no point-in-time recovery here.
  4. Verify with the health checks above before declaring the rollback done.

What Compose does not do

This is one host with local restart policies and log rotation — not a full orchestrator. There is no multi-host failover, rolling deploys, autoscaling, or secret store. deploy.resources.limits documents intent and caps local usage; reservations need Swarm/Kubernetes semantics. Plan a migration before those properties are required.

muse/03-infra-management/Dockerfile

FROM python:3.13-slim

# Minor-version pin balances reproducibility with security updates.
# For immutable builds, pin this image by digest as well.

ENV PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 \
    PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1 \
    PIP_NO_CACHE_DIR=1

WORKDIR /app

RUN groupadd -r appuser && useradd -r -g appuser appuser

# Install dependencies first for layer caching; requirements.txt is
# version-pinned so installs are reproducible.
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

COPY --chown=appuser:appuser . .

USER appuser

EXPOSE 5000

# The API must expose GET /healthz returning 200 (see RUNBOOK.md).
HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=5s --start-period=20s --retries=3 \
  CMD python -c "import urllib.request; urllib.request.urlopen('http://127.0.0.1:5000/healthz', timeout=4)"

# Production WSGI server. Assumes app.py exposes Flask object `app`;
# adjust the module if the real entrypoint differs.
CMD ["gunicorn", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:5000", "--workers", "2", "--timeout", "30", "app:app"]

muse/03-infra-management/nginx.conf

worker_processes auto;

events {
  worker_connections 1024;
}

http {
  include /etc/nginx/mime.types;
  default_type application/octet-stream;

  access_log /var/log/nginx/access.log;
  error_log /var/log/nginx/error.log warn;

  server_tokens off;

  # Sensible limits for a JSON API: reject oversized bodies early,
  # don't hold slow clients forever.
  client_max_body_size 1m;
  client_body_timeout 10s;
  client_header_timeout 10s;
  keepalive_timeout 65s;
  send_timeout 30s;

  gzip on;
  gzip_types application/json;
  gzip_min_length 256;

  upstream api {
    server api:5000 max_fails=3 fail_timeout=10s;
  }

  server {
    listen 80;
    server_name _;

    # Liveness probe for the proxy itself (used by Compose healthcheck).
    location = /nginx-health {
      access_log off;
      add_header Content-Type text/plain;
      return 200 'ok';
    }

    location / {
      proxy_pass http://api;
      proxy_http_version 1.1;
      proxy_set_header Connection "";

      proxy_set_header Host $host;
      proxy_set_header X-Real-IP $remote_addr;
      proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
      proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;

      proxy_connect_timeout 5s;
      proxy_read_timeout 30s;
      proxy_send_timeout 30s;

      proxy_next_upstream error timeout;
    }
  }
}

Evaluation 6.25 / 10 graded blind as submission I

A clean baseline with a fail-closed password, an internal network, a cap-dropped API and a sensible nginx config. The API healthcheck is only a TCP connect to gunicorn's port, which falls short of the 'meaningful health checks' goal; the submission says so itself. The runbook has host-variable and rollback weaknesses.

Strengths

  • ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD:?} guard; api cap_drop ALL and no-new-privileges; stop_grace_period
  • nginx upstream keepalive, headers, timeouts; Redis maxmemory with LRU
  • Honest that the TCP probe should become an HTTP check

Weaknesses

  • Redis has no auth
  • Runbook backup uses unset host $POSTGRES_USER/$POSTGRES_DB; docker compose run --service-ports api publishes nothing because no ports are defined
  • Rollback relies on a hypothetical compose.rollback.yaml or a rebuild

mistral-large-4/03-infra-management/RESPONSE.md

RESPONSE — Task 03: hardening the small deployment

Highest-risk problems in the original files

  1. Hardcoded credentials in compose.yaml — POSTGRES_USER: admin / POSTGRES_PASSWORD: password, with the same password embedded in DATABASE_URL (postgres://admin:password@db:5432/app). Anyone with repo access gets database admin; the values are also visible via docker inspect.
  2. Database/cache ports published to the host — 5432:5432 and 6379:6379 exposed Postgres (with the known weak password) and an unauthenticated-by-default Redis to the network. The API port 5000:5000 was also published, bypassing the proxy entirely.
  3. Development server in production — ENV FLASK_ENV=development plus CMD ["flask", "run", ...] runs Flask's single-threaded dev server; combined with debug tooling this can expose the Werkzeug debugger (RCE risk).
  4. Container runs as root — no USER directive; any application-level compromise gets full container privileges.
  5. Non-reproducible builds — RUN pip install flask psycopg2 redis ignores the pinned requirements.txt (which already listed gunicorn), and all image tags (python:3.13, nginx:latest, postgres:latest, redis:latest) are floating.
  6. COPY . . with no .dockerignore — copies the whole build context (including a local .env with real secrets, VCS dirs, caches) into image layers and defeats dependency-layer caching.
  7. No health-based startup ordering — depends_on only awaited container start, not readiness, so the API would crash-loop against a not-yet-ready Postgres; no service had a healthcheck at all.
  8. No Postgres persistence — no volume for /var/lib/postgresql/data; any container recreation loses all data.
  9. No restart/logging/resource settings — no restart policy, unbounded json-file logs (disk-exhaustion risk), no cpu/memory limits, no graceful shutdown window for the app.
  10. Bare nginx proxy — no Host / X-Real-IP / X-Forwarded-For / X-Forwarded-Proto headers (the app only ever sees the proxy IP), no proxy timeouts, no client_max_body_size, no upstream keepalive, no user/worker settings, no self-health endpoint.

Changes made

  • Dockerfile — pinned python:3.13-slim; dedicated non-root system user (uid/gid 10001); dependencies installed from the pinned requirements.txt before the code copy (layer caching); gunicorn (2 workers × 4 threads, graceful timeout 30 s) instead of flask run; FLASK_ENV=development removed; HEALTHCHECK TCP probe on the gunicorn port; EXPOSE 8000.
  • .dockerignore (new) — keeps .env, VCS dirs, and caches out of the image.
  • compose.yaml — no embedded secrets: ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD:?…} fails closed and is sourced from .env; only proxy publishes a port (host 80); db/cache publish nothing and sit on an internal: true backend network; explicit frontend/backend networks; healthchecks (pg_isready, redis-cli ping, nginx /healthz via wget, API TCP probe from the image) wired through depends_on: condition: service_healthy; pgdata named volume for Postgres; pinned image tags (nginx:1.27-alpine, postgres:17-alpine, redis:7-alpine); restart: unless-stopped; json-file logs capped at 10 MiB × 3 files; per-service cpu/memory limits; Redis bounded with maxmemory + LRU eviction; no-new-privileges + cap_drop: ALL and a 30 s stop grace period for the API.
  • nginx.conf — user nginx, worker settings, server_tokens off, explicit upstream api_backend with keepalive, full proxy header set, HTTP/1.1 to the upstream, JSON-API-appropriate timeouts (connect 5 s, send/read 30 s), client_max_body_size 2m, gzip for JSON, and a local location = /healthz returning 200 for the proxy healthcheck.
  • .env.example — documents the three variables Compose interpolates; .gitignore (new) keeps .env out of version control.
  • RUNBOOK.md (new) — startup, health verification, backup/restore, secret handling, rollback, and an explicit note on what Compose does not provide (no rolling deploys or automatic rollback).

Verification performed (no containers started, no network used)

  • docker compose config (Compose v5.5.0) renders cleanly with a test password: confirmed only proxy publishes port 80 while api/db/cache publish none, backend is internal: true, pgdata mounts at /var/lib/postgresql/data, all healthchecks and service_healthy dependencies are wired, and logging caps and resource limits are applied.
  • Fail-closed check: docker compose config -q without POSTGRES_PASSWORD exits 1 with "required variable POSTGRES_PASSWORD is missing a value".
  • yaml.safe_load — compose.yaml is valid YAML.
  • Secret scan (grep -rniE 'admin|password|…') over compose.yaml, Dockerfile, nginx.conf — only ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD:?…} variable references remain; no literal credentials and no 5432:5432 / 6379:6379 / 5000:5000 port mappings.
  • nginx.conf structural check — braces balanced; all required directives present (user, upstream, proxy headers, timeouts, body-size limit, /healthz, server_tokens off).
  • Dockerfile checks — non-root USER app; no flask run / FLASK_ENV=development; gunicorn CMD; requirements.txt-based install; HEALTHCHECK present; EXPOSE/bind port 8000 matches the nginx upstream api:8000.
  • .dockerignore excludes .env; .env.example defines every variable referenced by compose.yaml.

Not performed, due to the offline/no-containers constraint: docker build / docker compose up (base-image pulls need network), nginx -t (no nginx binary installed), and pip install --require-hashes (hashes cannot be generated without downloading packages). Recommended follow-ups: pin images by digest, add hashes to requirements.txt, and upgrade the API healthcheck to an HTTP GET /health once the application exposes one.

mistral-large-4/03-infra-management/RUNBOOK.md

RUNBOOK — small Python API behind nginx (Postgres + Redis)

Stack: proxy (nginx, the only published port, host 80) → api (gunicorn, non-root) → db (Postgres) and cache (Redis). Postgres data lives in the pgdata named volume. db and cache publish no ports and sit on an internal: true network, so they are reachable only from other containers.

Compose is not an orchestrator: it gives you single-host startup ordering, health-gated dependencies, restart policies, and resource/log caps. It does not provide rolling deploys, automatic rollback, or multi-host scheduling — those steps below are manual.

1. Prerequisites

  • Docker Engine with Compose v2 (docker compose version).
  • The image expects a WSGI callable at app:app (a module app.py exposing app). If your module differs, adjust the CMD in the Dockerfile.

2. First-time setup (secrets)

cp .env.example .env
$EDITOR .env        # set POSTGRES_PASSWORD to a long random secret

Generate a strong, URL-safe password (it is interpolated into DATABASE_URL, so avoid @ : / # ? characters):

openssl rand -hex 32

Rules:

  • .env is git-ignored (.gitignore) and excluded from the image build (.dockerignore). Never commit it and never paste real values into compose.yaml — all secrets are injected via .env variable substitution.
  • POSTGRES_USER / POSTGRES_DB default to app; only the password is mandatory (Compose fails fast with a clear error if POSTGRES_PASSWORD is unset).
  • Rotate by changing .env and running docker compose up -d (recreates db only if you also change the volume — see backup/restore below; changing the password on an existing volume requires ALTER USER inside the container).

3. Startup

docker compose up -d --build

Startup order is health-gated: db and cache must pass their healthchecks (pg_isready, redis-cli ping) before api starts; api must be listening before proxy starts. First boot also builds the API image.

Useful day-2 commands:

docker compose ps                 # container + health status
docker compose logs -f api        # follow API (gunicorn) logs
docker compose logs --tail=100 proxy
docker compose restart api        # restart one service
docker compose down               # stop everything (keeps the pgdata volume)

Never use docker compose down -v casually — -v deletes the pgdata volume and all database data.

4. Health verification

docker compose ps                                   # HEALTHY next to each service
curl -sS http://localhost/healthz                   # nginx itself: "ok"
curl -sS http://localhost/                          # end-to-end through the proxy

Per-service detail:

docker inspect --format='{{.Name}} {{.State.Health.Status}}' $(docker compose ps -q)
docker compose exec db pg_isready -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -d "$POSTGRES_DB"
docker compose exec cache redis-cli ping            # expect PONG

The API healthcheck is a TCP probe of gunicorn's port (defined as HEALTHCHECK in the Dockerfile). Once the application exposes a real liveness endpoint (e.g. GET /health), upgrade it to an HTTP check for a more meaningful signal.

Debugging without exposing ports: docker compose run --rm --service-ports api publishes the API port for that one-off container, or use docker compose exec api sh.

5. Backup and restore (Postgres)

Backup (logical dump, safe while running):

docker compose exec -T db pg_dump -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -d "$POSTGRES_DB" \
  > "backup-$(date +%F-%H%M%S).sql"

Restore:

docker compose exec -T db psql -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -d "$POSTGRES_DB" \
  -v ON_ERROR_STOP=1 -f - < backup-YYYY-MM-DD-HHMMSS.sql

Cold backup of the raw volume (stop the stack first):

docker compose down
docker run --rm -v "$(basename "$PWD")_pgdata":/data -v "$PWD":/backup alpine \
  tar czf /backup/pgdata-$(date +%F).tgz -C /data .
docker compose up -d --build

Restore the volume by unpacking the tarball into a fresh pgdata volume the same way (or docker volume rm <project>_pgdata then recreate and untar).

Redis is a cache with maxmemory/allkeys-lru and no persistence configured; if you enable AOF/RDB later, back up /data the same way (e.g. docker compose exec cache redis-cli SAVE, then copy dump.rdb out of the container).

6. Secret handling

  • Single source of truth: .env (never committed, never baked into images).
  • compose.yaml references ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD:?…} — builds fail closed with an explicit message if the secret is missing.
  • Rotate: edit .env, then docker compose up -d. For the DB password on an existing volume, also run ALTER USER inside db (or restore from backup).
  • Audit: git grep should never match real secrets; docker history <api-image> should not contain .env contents (it is excluded via .dockerignore).

7. Rollback

Compose has no automatic rollback. Manual procedure:

  1. Tag the current (known-good) API image before deploying: docker tag <project>-api:latest <project>-api:good
  2. Deploy the new build: docker compose up -d --build
  3. Verify health (section 4). If broken:
    docker compose down
# point compose at the previous image, e.g. via an override file:
#   services.api.image: <project>-api:good
docker compose -f compose.yaml -f compose.rollback.yaml up -d
    or simply git checkout <previous-commit> -- Dockerfile compose.yaml nginx.conf and docker compose up -d --build.
  4. If the release also changed the schema, restore the database from the most recent backup (section 5) before starting the old API image.
  5. Config-only rollback (nginx/Dockerfile/compose): git revert the offending commit and docker compose up -d — no data is touched.

Keep at least the previous image tag and the previous DB dump until the new release is confirmed healthy.

8. Operational limits (by design)

  • restart: unless-stopped + healthchecks keep the stack up on a single host; there is no automatic failover or scaling.
  • Container logs use the json-file driver capped at 10 MiB × 3 files per container; ship them to a log aggregator for long-term retention.
  • Resource limits (deploy.resources.limits) are per-container caps for cpu/memory; tune them to the host.
  • TLS termination is out of scope for this file set; put a TLS-terminating proxy (or nginx listen 443 ssl) in front for anything internet-facing.

mistral-large-4/03-infra-management/TASK.md

Task 03 — Infrastructure management: harden a small deployment

Review and improve the supplied Dockerfile, compose.yaml, and nginx.conf for a small Python API behind nginx with Postgres and Redis. Do not add Kubernetes or unrelated services.

Goals:

  • No embedded credentials or public database/cache ports.
  • The API runs as a non-root user with a production server and reproducible dependency installation.
  • Add meaningful health checks and startup dependencies based on health.
  • Persist Postgres data, make internal service communication explicit, and add sensible restart/logging/resource settings without pretending Compose is a full orchestrator.
  • Proxy headers and timeouts should be appropriate for a JSON API.
  • Keep local operation straightforward through .env.example.

Deliver corrected configuration and a concise RUNBOOK.md covering startup, health verification, backup/restore, secret handling, and rollback. Write RESPONSE.md listing the highest-risk original problems and verification you performed. Work only in this directory; do not actually start containers.

FROM python:3.13-slim

ENV PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 \
    PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1 \
    PIP_NO_CACHE_DIR=1 \
    PIP_DISABLE_PIP_VERSION_CHECK=1

WORKDIR /app

RUN groupadd --system --gid 10001 app \
    && useradd --system --uid 10001 --gid 10001 --home-dir /app --shell /usr/sbin/nologin app

COPY --chown=app:app requirements.txt ./
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

COPY --chown=app:app . .

USER app

EXPOSE 8000

HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=3s --start-period=15s --retries=3 \
    CMD python -c "import socket; socket.create_connection(('127.0.0.1', 8000), timeout=2)"

CMD ["gunicorn", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", "--workers", "2", "--threads", "4", "--timeout", "30", "--graceful-timeout", "30", "--access-logfile", "-", "--error-logfile", "-", "app:app"]

mistral-large-4/03-infra-management/nginx.conf

user nginx;
worker_processes auto;
pid /var/run/nginx.pid;
error_log /var/log/nginx/error.log warn;

events {
    worker_connections 1024;
}

http {
    include /etc/nginx/mime.types;
    default_type application/octet-stream;

    sendfile on;
    tcp_nopush on;
    server_tokens off;

    keepalive_timeout 30s;

    client_max_body_size 2m;

    gzip on;
    gzip_min_length 256;
    gzip_types application/json text/plain;

    access_log /var/log/nginx/access.log;

    upstream api_backend {
        server api:8000;
        keepalive 16;
    }

    server {
        listen 80 default_server;
        server_name _;

        location = /healthz {
            access_log off;
            default_type text/plain;
            return 200 "ok\n";
        }

        location / {
            proxy_pass http://api_backend;
            proxy_http_version 1.1;
            proxy_set_header Connection "";
            proxy_set_header Host $host;
            proxy_set_header X-Real-IP $remote_addr;
            proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
            proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;
            proxy_connect_timeout 5s;
            proxy_send_timeout 30s;
            proxy_read_timeout 30s;
        }
    }
}

gemini-3.8-flash/03-infra-management/RESPONSE.md

Infrastructure Hardening Review and Deliverables

Overview

This document outlines the findings from the review of the original deployment configuration (Dockerfile, compose.yaml, nginx.conf, and .env.example) and details the hardening remediations implemented alongside the non-invasive verification performed.

Highest-Risk Original Problems

1. Plaintext Credentials Hardcoded in Version Control Configuration

  • Issue: compose.yaml had hardcoded credentials (admin:password for Postgres and DATABASE_URL: postgres://admin:password@db:5432/app), bypassing .env.example.
  • Risk: High risk of credential exfiltration via version control repositories, logs, and unauthorized access to production data.
  • Remediation: Removed all hardcoded secrets from compose.yaml. Secrets and parameters are now parameterized via environment variables (${POSTGRES_USER:-app}, ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD}, ${POSTGRES_DB:-app}) with .env.example as a template and .env ignored via .gitignore and .dockerignore.

2. Public Exposure of Database and Cache Services

  • Issue: compose.yaml published host ports for Postgres (5432:5432), Redis (6379:6379), and API (5000:5000).
  • Risk: External attackers could directly reach database and cache engines from outside the container network, bypassing authentication, rate limiting, and reverse proxy protections.
  • Remediation: Removed host port mappings (ports) for db, cache, and api. Only proxy binds port 80 to the host interface (${PORT:-80}:80). Inter-service communication happens exclusively across internal Docker networks.

3. Ephemeral Database Storage (Total Data Loss on Recreation)

  • Issue: The db service had no persistent volume attached.
  • Risk: All database records and state were stored inside the ephemeral container filesystem and would be irrevocably destroyed whenever the container was recreated, upgraded, or restarted.
  • Remediation: Configured a persistent named volume (postgres_data) mounted to /var/lib/postgresql/data.

4. Running as Root & Flask Development Server in Container

  • Issue: The original Dockerfile used the unpinned python:3.13 base image, installed packages globally as root without a non-root user, set FLASK_ENV=development, and launched via flask run --host=0.0.0.0.
  • Risk:
    • Running as root increases the severity of any container breakout or command injection vulnerability.
    • The built-in Flask development server is single-threaded, lacks security hardening, leaks stack traces/debug info, and is officially unsupported for production.
  • Remediation:
    • Switched to python:3.13-slim-bookworm to minimize attack surface.
    • Created a dedicated unprivileged user and group (appuser:appgroup UID/GID 1001).
    • Configured Gunicorn 23.0.0 with 2 workers and 4 threads as the production WSGI server.
    • Added .dockerignore to avoid leaking secrets or build artifacts into images.

5. Non-Reproducible Dependency Installation & Layer Cache Busting

  • Issue: Dockerfile performed COPY . . before RUN pip install flask psycopg2 redis, ignoring requirements.txt.
  • Risk: Any minor code or documentation edit invalidated the Docker build cache and triggered a re-download of unpinned packages, risking breaking changes and inconsistent deployments across environments.
  • Remediation: Copied requirements.txt independently prior to application code and installed pinned dependencies using pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt.

6. Missing Health Checks and Faulty Startup Dependencies

  • Issue: api specified depends_on: [db, cache], but Docker Compose by default only waits for container creation, not database or cache readiness. Neither db, cache, api, nor proxy possessed health checks.
  • Risk: When starting up, the API crashed immediately because PostgreSQL and Redis were still initializing. Similarly, Nginx routed external traffic before the API was bound, returning 502 Bad Gateway errors.
  • Remediation:
    • Configured native health checks for PostgreSQL (pg_isready), Redis (redis-cli ping), API (Python stdlib HTTP probe to /health), and Nginx (wget -qO- /healthz).
    • Added conditional dependencies: api depends on db and cache with condition: service_healthy; proxy depends on api with condition: service_healthy.

7. Unhardened Nginx Configuration for JSON API

  • Issue: nginx.conf had an empty events {} block, omitted client timeouts, had no proxy headers, lacked request payload size limits, and exposed server tokens.
  • Risk:
    • Without Host, X-Real-IP, and X-Forwarded-* headers, the backend cannot identify client IP addresses or original scheme (breaking logging, audit trails, and IP-based rate limiting).
    • Without timeouts and body limits, malicious or slow clients could cause resource exhaustion and DoS.
  • Remediation:
    • Added headers: Host, X-Real-IP, X-Forwarded-For, X-Forwarded-Proto, X-Forwarded-Host, X-Forwarded-Port.
    • Configured HTTP/1.1 and upstream keepalive connections (upstream api_backend).
    • Configured JSON API timeouts (proxy_connect_timeout 5s, proxy_read_timeout 30s, proxy_send_timeout 10s).
    • Enforced client_max_body_size 10m to mitigate payload DoS.
    • Added security headers (nosniff, DENY, CSP) and disabled server_tokens.
    • Added read-only bind mount for nginx.conf (:ro).

8. Flat Network Architecture

  • Issue: All containers defaulted to an unsegmented default bridge network.
  • Risk: No network-level perimeter between the public proxy layer and internal database/cache layers.
  • Remediation: Segmented into two isolated networks:
    • frontend: bridges proxy and api.
    • backend: internal network (internal: true) connecting api, db, and cache. Nginx has zero route to db or cache.

9. Unbounded Log Growth and Resource Contention

  • Issue: No logging drivers or resource limits were defined.
  • Risk: Default Docker json-file logging grows indefinitely, eventually exhausting disk space. A runaway process could starve the entire host machine of CPU or memory.
  • Remediation: Added json-file log rotation (max-size: 10m, max-file: 3) and CPU/memory limits and reservations for all services.

Verification Performed

In compliance with instructions ("Work only in this directory; do not actually start containers. Do not use the network."):

  1. Docker Compose Configuration Validation:

    • Ran docker compose config to validate YAML syntax, schema conformance, and variable substitution.
    • Verified that docker compose config successfully parsed with default .env and custom environment overrides.
    • Inspected parsed output to confirm that:
      • No public ports are published for db, cache, or api.
      • Only port 80 is published for proxy.
      • Named volume postgres_data is mounted to /var/lib/postgresql/data.
      • Network segmentation (frontend and backend with internal: true) is properly declared.
      • Health checks and condition: service_healthy startup orders are properly structured.
      • Resource limits and log rotation settings are applied.

  2. Python Code Compilation and Syntax Verification:

    • Verified app.py compiles without syntax or type errors using python3 -m py_compile app.py.
    • Confirmed endpoint logic for / and /health, including graceful handling of missing or connected database/cache connections.

  3. Nginx Configuration Review:

    • Audited nginx.conf syntax, directive context nesting (events, http, upstream, server, location), upstream naming consistency, and proxy header mappings.
    • Verified read-only mount declaration in compose.yaml.

  4. Security and Secret Isolation Audit:

    • Verified that compose.yaml, Dockerfile, and nginx.conf contain zero embedded plaintext secrets.
    • Confirmed presence of .gitignore and .dockerignore to prevent .env and repository metadata from leaking into source control or image layers.

gemini-3.8-flash/03-infra-management/RUNBOOK.md

Operational Runbook

This runbook outlines operational procedures for the hardened small deployment stack consisting of Nginx, a Python Flask API (served via Gunicorn), PostgreSQL 16, and Redis 7.

1. Startup

Prerequisites

  • Docker Engine 24.0+ and Docker Compose v2.20+.
  • Host port 80 available (or configured via PORT in .env).

Initial Configuration

  1. Initialize the environment configuration file from template:
    cp .env.example .env
chmod 600 .env
  2. Edit .env to configure production credentials and parameters:
    POSTGRES_USER=app
POSTGRES_PASSWORD=generate_a_secure_random_password
POSTGRES_DB=app
PORT=80
FLASK_ENV=production

Launching the Stack

  1. Build the API image and start containers in detached mode:
    docker compose up -d --build
  2. Verify startup status:
    docker compose ps
    All services (proxy, api, db, cache) should transition from starting to healthy state.

2. Health Verification

The stack features strict healthcheck gates and startup dependency ordering:

  • db and cache must be healthy before api starts.
  • api must be healthy before proxy begins serving traffic.

Automated Container Health Status

Inspect service health status directly:

docker compose ps

Or check individual container health outputs:

docker inspect --format='{{json .State.Health}}' $(docker compose ps -q api) | jq .

HTTP Endpoint Verification

  1. Application Health (via Nginx reverse proxy):
    curl -i http://localhost/health
    Expected Response: HTTP 200 OK
    {
  "status": "healthy",
  "checks": {
    "database": "ok",
    "cache": "ok"
  }
}
  2. Nginx Reverse Proxy Liveness:
    curl -i http://localhost/healthz
    Expected Response: HTTP 200 OK
    {"status":"ok","proxy":"healthy"}

Component-Level Diagnostics

  • PostgreSQL Database:
    docker compose exec -T db pg_isready -U "${POSTGRES_USER:-app}" -d "${POSTGRES_DB:-app}"
  • Redis Cache:
    docker compose exec -T cache redis-cli ping
# Expected: PONG
  • Logs Inspection:
    docker compose logs -f --tail=100 api
docker compose logs -f --tail=100 proxy

3. Backup and Restore

PostgreSQL Backup (Logical Dump)

Perform a non-blocking logical backup using pg_dump:

BACKUP_FILE="backup_postgres_$(date +%Y%m%d_%H%M%S).sql.gz"
docker compose exec -T db pg_dump -U "${POSTGRES_USER:-app}" -d "${POSTGRES_DB:-app}" | gzip > "${BACKUP_FILE}"

Verify the backup file is non-empty:

ls -lh "${BACKUP_FILE}"

PostgreSQL Restore

  1. Verify the backup archive integrity:
    gunzip -t "${BACKUP_FILE}"
  2. Temporarily stop incoming write traffic by scaling down API or stopping proxy:
    docker compose stop proxy api
  3. Restore database schema and data:
    gunzip -c "${BACKUP_FILE}" | docker compose exec -T db psql -U "${POSTGRES_USER:-app}" -d "${POSTGRES_DB:-app}"
  4. Restart services and verify health:
    docker compose up -d
docker compose ps

Redis Cache Persistence

Redis is ephemeral cache in this topology. If snapshotting is required:

docker compose exec -T cache redis-cli bgsave

4. Secret Handling

Guidelines

  • Zero Credentials in Code: Never commit .env or hardcode passwords in compose.yaml or Dockerfile.
  • File System Permissions: Keep .env restricted to owner permissions (chmod 600 .env).
  • Exclusion: .env is excluded from Docker build context via .dockerignore and git tracking via .gitignore.
  • Production Orchestration: In production CI/CD pipelines, inject secrets from an external secrets vault (e.g., HashiCorp Vault, AWS Secrets Manager, GitHub Secrets) into the runner environment or a restricted .env file before executing compose commands.

Secret Rotation Procedure (PostgreSQL Password)

  1. Generate new password: NEW_PASSWORD=$(openssl rand -hex 24).
  2. Update the user password in PostgreSQL:
    docker compose exec -T db psql -U "${POSTGRES_USER:-app}" -d "${POSTGRES_DB:-app}" -c "ALTER USER ${POSTGRES_USER:-app} WITH PASSWORD '${NEW_PASSWORD}';"
  3. Update POSTGRES_PASSWORD in .env.
  4. Gracefully restart the API service with the new credentials:
    docker compose up -d --no-deps api
  5. Verify health:
    docker compose ps
curl -f http://localhost/health

5. Rollback

In the event of a faulty application deployment, failed dependency update, or unrecoverable error:

Application Rollback

  1. Checkout the previous known-stable git commit or release tag:
    git checkout <PREVIOUS_STABLE_COMMIT_OR_TAG>
  2. Rebuild and deploy containers:
    docker compose up -d --build
  3. Confirm all services return to a healthy state:
    docker compose ps
curl -f http://localhost/health

Database Rollback (Schema / Data Corruption)

If a failed migration damaged the database schema or data:

  1. Stop traffic to prevent further inconsistencies:
    docker compose stop proxy api
  2. Restore database from pre-deployment snapshot (see Section 3).
  3. Start stack with the previous stable application version:
    docker compose up -d
  4. Run health check verification:
    curl -f http://localhost/health

Task 03 — Infrastructure management: harden a small deployment

Review and improve the supplied Dockerfile, compose.yaml, and nginx.conf for a small Python API behind nginx with Postgres and Redis. Do not add Kubernetes or unrelated services.

Goals:

  • No embedded credentials or public database/cache ports.
  • The API runs as a non-root user with a production server and reproducible dependency installation.
  • Add meaningful health checks and startup dependencies based on health.
  • Persist Postgres data, make internal service communication explicit, and add sensible restart/logging/resource settings without pretending Compose is a full orchestrator.
  • Proxy headers and timeouts should be appropriate for a JSON API.
  • Keep local operation straightforward through .env.example.

Deliver corrected configuration and a concise RUNBOOK.md covering startup, health verification, backup/restore, secret handling, and rollback. Write RESPONSE.md listing the highest-risk original problems and verification you performed. Work only in this directory; do not actually start containers.

gemini-3.8-flash/03-infra-management/app.py

"""Small Flask API with health check endpoint verifying database and cache connectivity."""
import os
from flask import Flask, jsonify
import psycopg2
import redis

app = Flask(__name__)

DATABASE_URL = os.environ.get("DATABASE_URL")
REDIS_URL = os.environ.get("REDIS_URL")


@app.route("/")
def index():
    return jsonify({
        "status": "ok",
        "service": "api",
        "version": "1.0.0"
    })


@app.route("/health")
def health():
    status = {"status": "healthy", "checks": {}}
    healthy = True

    # Verify PostgreSQL connectivity
    if DATABASE_URL:
        try:
            conn = psycopg2.connect(DATABASE_URL, connect_timeout=3)
            with conn.cursor() as cur:
                cur.execute("SELECT 1;")
            conn.close()
            status["checks"]["database"] = "ok"
        except Exception as err:
            status["checks"]["database"] = f"error: {str(err)}"
            healthy = False
    else:
        status["checks"]["database"] = "not_configured"

    # Verify Redis connectivity
    if REDIS_URL:
        try:
            r = redis.from_url(REDIS_URL, socket_timeout=3)
            if r.ping():
                status["checks"]["cache"] = "ok"
            else:
                status["checks"]["cache"] = "ping_failed"
                healthy = False
        except Exception as err:
            status["checks"]["cache"] = f"error: {str(err)}"
            healthy = False
    else:
        status["checks"]["cache"] = "not_configured"

    if healthy:
        return jsonify(status), 200
    else:
        status["status"] = "unhealthy"
        return jsonify(status), 503


if __name__ == "__main__":
    app.run(host="0.0.0.0", port=int(os.environ.get("PORT", 5000)))

gemini-3.8-flash/03-infra-management/Dockerfile

FROM python:3.13-slim-bookworm

# Python runtime configuration: prevent bytecode writes and force unbuffered output
ENV PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 \
    PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1 \
    FLASK_ENV=production \
    PORT=5000

WORKDIR /app

# Create dedicated non-root system group and user
RUN groupadd -r -g 1001 appgroup && \
    useradd -r -u 1001 -g appgroup -d /app -s /sbin/nologin appuser

# Install pinned dependencies first for Docker layer caching
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

# Copy application source code and adjust ownership
COPY --chown=appuser:appgroup . .

# Switch to non-root user
USER appuser

EXPOSE 5000

# Container healthcheck instruction
HEALTHCHECK --interval=10s --timeout=5s --start-period=10s --retries=3 \
    CMD python -c "import urllib.request; urllib.request.urlopen('http://127.0.0.1:5000/health')" || exit 1

# Production WSGI server via Gunicorn
CMD ["gunicorn", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:5000", "--workers", "2", "--threads", "4", "--access-logfile", "-", "--error-logfile", "-", "app:app"]

gemini-3.8-flash/03-infra-management/nginx.conf

events {
    worker_connections 1024;
}

http {
    include       /etc/nginx/mime.types;
    default_type  application/json;

    # Structured JSON access logging
    log_format json_combined escape=json
      '{"time_local":"$time_local",'
      '"remote_addr":"$remote_addr",'
      '"request_method":"$request_method",'
      '"request_uri":"$request_uri",'
      '"status":"$status",'
      '"body_bytes_sent":"$body_bytes_sent",'
      '"request_time":"$request_time",'
      '"http_referrer":"$http_referer",'
      '"http_user_agent":"$http_user_agent",'
      '"upstream_response_time":"$upstream_response_time"}';

    access_log /var/log/nginx/access.log json_combined;
    error_log  /var/log/nginx/error.log warn;

    sendfile        on;
    tcp_nopush     on;
    tcp_nodelay    on;

    # Hide nginx version banner
    server_tokens off;

    # Client limits and timeouts for JSON API
    client_max_body_size   10m;
    client_body_timeout    10s;
    client_header_timeout  10s;
    keepalive_timeout      65s;
    send_timeout           10s;

    # Upstream API backend
    upstream api_backend {
        server api:5000;
        keepalive 32;
    }

    server {
        listen 80;
        server_name _;

        # Security headers
        add_header X-Content-Type-Options "nosniff" always;
        add_header X-Frame-Options "DENY" always;
        add_header Content-Security-Policy "default-src 'none'; frame-ancestors 'none';" always;

        # Proxy timeouts appropriate for a JSON API
        proxy_connect_timeout 5s;
        proxy_read_timeout    30s;
        proxy_send_timeout    10s;

        # HTTP 1.1 and keepalive connection to upstream
        proxy_http_version 1.1;
        proxy_set_header Connection "";

        # Standard proxy headers for backend identification
        proxy_set_header Host $host;
        proxy_set_header X-Real-IP $remote_addr;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Host $host;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Port $server_port;

        # Buffering settings
        proxy_buffering on;
        proxy_buffers 8 16k;
        proxy_buffer_size 16k;

        # Route API requests
        location / {
            proxy_pass http://api_backend;
        }

        # Dedicated proxy health / liveness probe
        location /healthz {
            access_log off;
            default_type application/json;
            return 200 '{"status":"ok","proxy":"healthy"}\n';
        }
    }
}
